PKM design can tighten this coupling by embedding regulatory responses in monitoring mechanisms (e.g., spaced repetition adjusting review difficulty automatically) and make it visible through logging both monitoring events and regulatory responses.
> **Source:** Section IV
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #monitoring-control-coupling, #pkm-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 8 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** List the four phases of the Metacognitive PKM Lifecycle and the dominant metacognitive characteristic of each.
> **A:** (1) *Unconscious Capture* — metacognitive monitoring dominated by fluency illusions; regulation ad hoc. (2) *Structured Processing* — external scaffolding provides regulatory structure; monitoring improves through retrieval practice. (3) *Reflective Integration* — practitioner monitors and regulates *system use* itself; calibration improves. (4) *Metacognitive Mastery* — flexible, context-sensitive regulation; conscious management of the internal-external metacognition balance.
> **Source:** Section VII
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #lifecycle, #development, #pkm

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 9 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is cognitive offloading, and what is the "PKM calibration paradox" it creates?
> **A:** Cognitive offloading is using external resources to reduce internal cognitive demands. The PKM calibration paradox: creating comprehensive notes can *worsen* calibration because the practitioner mistakes *access fluency* (knowing where to find information in their system) for *retrieval fluency* (actually knowing the information). The notes serve as external memory that the practitioner mistakenly attributes to their internal memory, inflating confidence.
> **Source:** Section V
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #cognitive-offloading, #calibration, #paradox

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Flashcard 10 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does the parity principle from the extended mind thesis apply to metacognitive tools?
> **A:** The parity principle states: if an external process functions in a way that, were it done internally, we would count it as cognitive, then it IS cognitive regardless of physical location. Applied to metacognition: if a PKM system's spaced repetition algorithm performs the same function as an internal FOK judgment (assessing whether knowledge is sufficiently consolidated for retrieval), the parity principle suggests we should count it as a metacognitive process — making the system part of the agent's metacognitive architecture.
> **Source:** Section VI
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #parity-principle, #extended-mind, #metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 1 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is metacognitive knowledge, and how does it differ from metacognitive regulation?
> **A:** Metacognitive knowledge is stored, consciously accessible beliefs about cognitive processes, strategies, and states — the *content* of the metacognitive system residing in long-term memory. Metacognitive regulation refers to the executive processes (planning, monitoring, evaluating) that govern cognitive activity in real time. Knowledge provides the content from which regulation draws; regulation is the operational use of that content.
> **Source:** Phase II.1
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #metacognition #metacognitive-knowledge #definition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 2 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between declarative, procedural, and conditional metacognitive knowledge?
> **A:** Declarative = knowing *that* (a strategy exists and what it does); Procedural = knowing *how* (the operational steps for executing a strategy); Conditional = knowing *when and why* (the contextual conditions under which a strategy is effective or ineffective). Conditional knowledge is the most sophisticated, most domain-specific, and most frequently neglected in instruction.
> **Source:** Phase II.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognitive-knowledge #declarative #procedural #conditional

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 3 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three dimensions of Flavell's metacognitive knowledge taxonomy?
> **A:** Person knowledge (beliefs about human cognition in general, specific others' cognition, and one's own cognitive profile); Task knowledge (beliefs about how task characteristics affect cognitive demands); Strategy knowledge (beliefs about cognitive and metacognitive strategies: what they are, how they work, when they are effective).
> **Source:** Phase II.1
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #metacognitive-knowledge #Flavell #person-task-strategy

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 4 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What is the monitoring-control hierarchy in the Nelson-Narens model, and what role does metacognitive knowledge play in it?
> **A:** The model distinguishes an object level (where cognitive work occurs) and a meta-level (where processes are represented and managed). Monitoring flows upward (object → meta: the meta-level receives information about current processing). Control flows downward (meta → object: the meta-level modifies processing). Metacognitive knowledge provides the meta-level's representational content: the standards for monitoring and the inventory of control responses.
> **Source:** Phase III.3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #Nelson-Narens #monitoring #control #metacognitive-knowledge

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 5 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What are metacognitive experiences (Efklides), and how do they differ from metacognitive knowledge?
> **A:** Metacognitive experiences are online, phenomenological, affective-cognitive states that arise during task processing (feelings of knowing, difficulty, confidence, familiarity). They are situationally specific, affectively toned, and generated by heuristic processes (fluency, familiarity). Metacognitive knowledge is stored, stable, propositional, and retrievable. Metacognitive experiences serve as the primary triggers for regulatory responses and as the interface through which stored knowledge is engaged with ongoing activity.
> **Source:** Phase II.3, Phase III.4
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognitive-experience #metacognitive-feelings #Efklides #metacognitive-knowledge

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 6 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Knowledge-Activation Gap, and what mechanisms are hypothesized to produce it?
> **A:** The Knowledge-Activation Gap is the systematic failure of stored, declaratively accurate metacognitive knowledge to activate in relevant regulatory contexts during task performance. Three mechanisms are proposed: (1) indexing insufficiency (knowledge indexed to instruction contexts, not task-embedded monitoring signals); (2) proceduralization deficit (insufficient supported practice to proceduralize declarative knowledge); (3) monitoring-knowledge decoupling (weak associative links between monitoring state representations and metacognitive knowledge elements). [Note: speculative synthesis requiring validation]
> **Source:** Phase IV.3
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #knowledge-activation-gap #metacognitive-knowledge #regulation #original-construct

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 7 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** How does metacognitive knowledge contribute differently to each phase of Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model?
> **A:** Forethought Phase: Person + Task + Strategy knowledge supports accurate task analysis, goal-setting, and strategic planning. Performance Phase: Procedural + conditional strategy knowledge supports monitoring standards and control responses. Self-Reflection Phase: Declarative + conditional knowledge supports accurate causal attribution and strategy evaluation, enabling productive updating of metacognitive knowledge from experience.
> **Source:** Phase IV.4
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #SRL #Zimmerman #forethought #performance #self-reflection #metacognitive-knowledge

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 8 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is pseudometacognition, and how is it distinguishable from genuine metacognitive knowledge?
> **A:** Pseudometacognition is metacognitive-appearing knowledge or behavior that does not reflect genuine awareness of one's actual cognitive processes or accurately calibrated monitoring. It is characterized by context-insensitivity (same narrative regardless of task/performance variation) and failure to generate adaptive regulatory responses when monitoring signals indicate need. Distinguished from genuine metacognitive knowledge by: insensitivity to performance feedback, failure to update under disconfirming evidence, and absence of corresponding regulatory improvement.
> **Source:** Phase V.3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #pseudometacognition #metacognitive-knowledge #calibration #monitoring

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 9 — Evidence
> **A:** **Q:** What does calibration research reveal about the accuracy of metacognitive monitoring, and how can calibration be improved?
> **A:** Calibration research consistently shows overconfident monitoring in novice and moderately experienced learners in domains with delayed or indirect feedback. The fluency illusion (smooth processing mistaken for deep comprehension) is the most common mechanism. Calibration is improvable through: retrieval practice (experience of recognition-recall gap recalibrates predictions), delayed JOLs (delayed self-assessments are more accurate than immediate ones), and explicit instruction in sources of miscalibration.
> **Source:** Phase IV.5
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #calibration #metacognitive-calibration #fluency-illusion #retrieval-practice

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 10 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** Why is conditional metacognitive knowledge the most domain-specific dimension of the taxonomy, and what are the implications for transfer?
> **A:** Conditional knowledge (knowing when and why a strategy is effective) requires knowledge of the specific task structures, epistemic conventions, and performance standards of a domain to be genuinely functional. Generic conditions (e.g., "use elaborative interrogation for expository text") are only marginally functional — truly conditional knowledge specifies conditions at the level of text genre, comprehension goal, prior knowledge threshold, and task demand. Transfer of conditional metacognitive knowledge across domains is poor because these conditions are domain-indexed.
> **Source:** Phase III.6
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #conditional-metacognitive-knowledge #domain-specificity #transfer #metacognitive-knowledge

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 11 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** How does the domain-generality debate in metacognitive knowledge research resolve, and what are the implications for instruction?
> **A:** Evidence supports a layered model: declarative metacognitive knowledge (broad cognitive principles) is domain-general and transfers; conditional metacognitive knowledge is domain-specific and transfers poorly. Implication: generic metacognitive workshops improve declarative metacognitive knowledge but not conditional; metacognitive instruction must be domain-embedded for conditional knowledge development. The general principles do not substitute for domain-specific conditional knowledge but accelerate its acquisition by providing conceptual frameworks for organizing domain-specific experience.
> **Source:** Phase III.6
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #metacognitive-knowledge #domain-generality #domain-specificity #transfer #instruction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 12 — Mechanism
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three pathways through which metacognitive knowledge is acquired?
> **A:** (1) Experience-based acquisition: patterns of success/failure encoded as conditional knowledge through direct task experience — slow, susceptible to systematic biases, dependent on accurate feedback. (2) Instruction-based acquisition: explicit transmission of declarative metacognitive knowledge, providing conceptual categories that structure subsequent experience. (3) Social transmission: expert-novice dialogue, observational learning, and internalization of regulatory speech (Vygotsky) — transmits conditional knowledge that would otherwise require years to develop experientially.
> **Source:** Phase IV.1
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognitive-knowledge #acquisition #experience #instruction #social-transmission

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 13 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** What distinguishes effective from ineffective metacognitive knowledge instruction?
> **A:** Effective metacognitive knowledge instruction: (1) is explicit — does not rely on implicit development alone; (2) is domain-embedded — develops conditional knowledge within domain-specific content; (3) targets the conditional dimension explicitly — addresses when and why, not just what and how; (4) is feedback-rich — structures retrieval-based feedback that makes calibration errors visible; (5) practices metacognitive knowledge in the contextual conditions where it should activate, not only in decontextualized instruction.
> **Source:** Phase V.1
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognitive-knowledge #instruction #educational-application #conditional #feedback

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 14 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** Why is the retrieval timing of metacognitive judgments of learning significant for calibration accuracy?
> **A:** Immediate JOLs are dominated by fluency and familiarity effects from recent processing — material that was just studied is highly accessible, producing inflated confidence. Delayed JOLs (made after a delay allowing processing-fluency effects to dissipate) are substantially more accurate predictors of actual retention because they depend more on genuine memory strength signals and less on transient fluency. This implies learners should use delayed JOLs and retrieval-based tests rather than rereading fluency as mastery indicators.
> **Source:** Phase IV.5
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #JOL #judgment-of-learning #calibration #fluency-illusion #retrieval-practice

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 15 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does Ann Brown's knowledge-regulation distinction relate to the Knowledge-Activation Gap construct proposed in this report?
> **A:** Brown's distinction (knowledge of cognition vs. regulation of cognition) identifies the phenomenon — that accurate metacognitive knowledge and effective metacognitive regulation can dissociate — but does not specify the mechanisms of dissociation. The Knowledge-Activation Gap construct attempts to specify three mechanisms that could produce this dissociation: indexing insufficiency, proceduralization deficit, and monitoring-knowledge decoupling. The Knowledge-Activation Gap is thus a mechanistic elaboration of Brown's empirical observation, though its status remains speculative.
> **Source:** Phase III.2, Phase IV.3
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #Brown #knowledge-regulation #knowledge-activation-gap #metacognitive-knowledge

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What distinguishes metacognitive reflection from metacognitive monitoring?
> **A:** Metacognitive monitoring is continuous, often automatic, and online (occurring during task performance); it generates signals about current cognitive state. Metacognitive reflection is episodic, effortful, and offline; it operates on accumulated monitoring outputs to evaluate patterns, generate explanatory accounts, and revise metacognitive knowledge.
> **Source:** Section 3 — The Monitoring-Control Framework
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** metacognition, definitions, monitoring, reflection

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Name and define the four principal metacognitive monitoring constructs in the Nelson-Narens framework.
> **A:** (1) Ease of Learning (EOL): pre-study prediction of learning difficulty. (2) Judgment of Learning (JOL): during/post-study prediction of future recall. (3) Feeling of Knowing (FOK): sense that unrecalled information is in memory and accessible. (4) Retrospective Confidence Judgment: post-response assessment of answer accuracy.
> **Source:** Section 3 — Monitoring Constructs
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** Nelson-Narens, monitoring, calibration

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fluency illusion and why is it particularly dangerous for metacognitive reflection?
> **A:** The fluency illusion is the error of interpreting ease of cognitive processing (fluency) as evidence of genuine understanding or learning. It is dangerous because it makes monitoring-based self-assessments unreliable — learners consistently generate overconfident JOLs for fluently processed material, terminate study prematurely, and correctly feel they understand material that retrieval testing reveals they cannot generate.
> **Source:** Section 5 — Failure Modes; Section 4 — Metacognitive Experience
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** fluency-illusion, calibration, distortions

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three sub-types of metacognitive knowledge, and what is the relationship between them?
> **A:** Declarative MK: factual knowledge *about* cognitive functioning (what influences performance). Procedural MK: knowledge *how* to use strategies and regulatory processes. Conditional MK: knowledge of *when and why* to deploy particular strategies for particular task types. The relationship is hierarchical-functional: declarative MK provides the knowledge base; procedural MK operationalizes it; conditional MK governs its deployment. All three are necessary for effective metacognitive reflection.
> **Source:** Section 4 — Metacognitive Knowledge
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** metacognitive-knowledge, declarative, procedural, conditional

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** According to Zimmerman's SRL model, why is attributional framing the pivotal variable in the self-reflection phase?
> **A:** Because the same performance outcome can generate adaptive or maladaptive reflective responses depending on what it is attributed to. Attribution to controllable, internal, unstable factors (strategy, effort) generates specific revision hypotheses: what to change to improve. Attribution to stable, uncontrollable factors (fixed ability) terminates productive reflection by positing a cause that cannot be modified; furthermore, it damages self-efficacy and reduces motivation for future learning engagement.
> **Source:** Section 6 — Zimmerman and Attribution Theory
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** SRL, Zimmerman, attribution, self-efficacy

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the distinction between reflection-in-action and reflection-on-action, and what is the developmental relationship between them?
> **A:** Reflection-in-action: metacognitive reflection occurring *during* practice when unexpected outcomes arise, involving on-the-spot reframing and experimental response. Reflection-on-action: retrospective articulation of tacit knowing-in-action *after* a practice episode, with more deliberate processing time. Developmentally, practitioners typically develop reflection-on-action capacity first; reflection-in-action capacity emerges with greater expertise as the practitioner develops faster, more automated access to tacit knowing and its anomalies.
> **Source:** Section 7 — Professional Contexts (Schön)
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** Schön, professional-practice, reflection

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the "Reflective Integration Cycle" proposed in this report, and what are its four stages?
> **A:** A proposed model of how metacognitive knowledge, monitoring outputs, and regulation interact during reflection: (1) Signal Collection: gathering accumulated monitoring outputs (JOLs, FOKs, difficulties) from a learning episode. (2) Pattern Recognition: identifying systematic patterns in those signals (consistent gaps, calibration errors, strategy misfires). (3) Explanatory Attribution: generating explanatory accounts for patterns (attributing to controllable, modifiable factors). (4) Metacognitive Knowledge Revision: updating declarative, procedural, and conditional MK with new insights.
> **Source:** Section 4 — Original Synthesis
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** original-synthesis, RIC, metacognitive-knowledge

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** How does the concept of "epistemic sovereignty" synthesize the key themes of metacognitive reflection?
> **A:** Epistemic sovereignty names the capacity to conduct one's cognitive life on the basis of reasoned and calibrated self-knowledge rather than unreflected habit, external prescription, or accident of early training. It connects metacognitive reflection to self-directed learning and lifelong learning by framing reflection as the primary mechanism through which cognitive autonomy is achieved — moving from cognitively-reactive (responding to environmental demands without self-knowledge) to cognitively-self-directed (deploying well-understood cognitive strategies based on accurate metacognitive identity).
> **Source:** Section: Synthesis — Epistemic Sovereignty
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** original-synthesis, epistemic-sovereignty, lifelong-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Name three evidence-based strategies specifically designed to bypass the fluency illusion in metacognitive reflection.
> **A:** (1) Free recall testing before re-reading — forces retrieval rather than recognition, revealing actual memory traces. (2) Generation before verification — attempting to explain a concept fully before consulting source. (3) Transfer application — attempting to apply a principle to a novel problem type, forcing generative understanding often inaccessible to recognition-based assessment.
> **Source:** Section 5 — Warning callout on Fluency Illusion Bypasses
> **Difficulty:** Basic-to-Intermediate
> **Tags:** practical, calibration, retrieval-practice

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the monitoring-regulation coupling, and what are its three common failure modes?
> **A:** Monitoring-regulation coupling is the relationship between the meta-level's acquisition of information about object-level state (monitoring) and the resulting commands that modify object-level processing (control). Three failure modes: (1) Monitoring-control decoupling: accurate monitoring without appropriate regulatory response (knowing but not acting). (2) Low-resolution monitoring: crude signals ("understand/don't understand") producing blunt regulatory responses. (3) Monitoring delay: detecting comprehension failures too late for proactive rather than reactive correction.
> **Source:** Section 3 — Monitoring-Regulation Coupling
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** monitoring, regulation, coupling, failure-modes

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Definition: Meta-Level
> **A:** **Q:** In the Nelson–Narrens model, what is the meta-level and what are its two primary functions?
> **A:** The meta-level is the higher-order component that contains a dynamic model of the object-level's state. Its two primary functions are monitoring (receiving information from the object-level through cue-mediated channels) and control (issuing directives that modify object-level processing).
> **Source:** Section I
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #metacognition #nelson-narrens #definition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Distinction: Monitoring vs. Control
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fundamental distinction between monitoring and control in the Nelson–Narrens architecture?
> **A:** Monitoring is the upward flow of information from the object-level to the meta-level — it *informs* the meta-level about the state of cognitive processing. Control is the downward flow from the meta-level to the object-level — it *modifies* cognitive processing based on monitoring information. The relationship is asymmetric: monitoring describes, control prescribes.
> **Source:** Sections I, III, IV
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #metacognition #monitoring #control

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Process: The Delayed JOL Effect
> **A:** **Q:** Why are delayed judgments of learning more accurate than immediate JOLs?
> **A:** Immediate JOLs are based on short-term memory cues (especially fluency from recency), which are non-diagnostic of long-term retention. Delayed JOLs force the learner to attempt retrieval from long-term memory, and the success or failure of that retrieval attempt is a highly diagnostic cue for future recall. The delay shifts the cue basis from unreliable to reliable cues.
> **Source:** Section V
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #JOL #delayed-JOL #cue-utilisation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Distinction: Calibration vs. Resolution
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between calibration and resolution in metacognitive monitoring?
> **A:** Calibration is the correspondence between absolute confidence and actual performance (e.g., 70% confidence → 70% accuracy). Resolution is the ability to discriminate between items that will and will not be recalled, regardless of absolute confidence values. A student can have poor calibration (systematic overconfidence) but good resolution (accurately ranking items by difficulty).
> **Source:** Section VI
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #calibration #resolution #monitoring-accuracy

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Connection: Nelson–Narrens and SRL
> **A:** **Q:** How does the Nelson–Narrens model relate to Zimmerman's cyclical model of self-regulated learning?
> **A:** The Nelson–Narrens model functions as the cognitive core embedded within Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model. Zimmerman's forethought phase maps onto pre-acquisition monitoring (EOLs), his performance phase onto concurrent monitoring (JOLs) and online control (strategy selection), and his self-reflection phase onto retrospective monitoring and subsequent control adjustment. Zimmerman adds motivational and affective dimensions that the Nelson–Narrens model does not address.
> **Source:** Section VII
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #SRL #zimmerman #integration

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Application: Cue Utilisation Theory
> **A:** **Q:** According to Koriat's cue utilisation approach, what three types of cues drive metacognitive monitoring judgments?
> **A:** (1) Intrinsic cues — inherent properties of the material (e.g., word frequency, concreteness, semantic relatedness). (2) Extrinsic cues — study conditions and strategies (e.g., number of repetitions, encoding conditions). (3) Mnemonic cues — internal signals during retrieval (e.g., processing fluency, partial accessibility, speed of retrieval).
> **Source:** Section III
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #cue-utilisation #koriat #monitoring

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Application: Region of Proximal Learning
> **A:** **Q:** What is the region of proximal learning, and what is the optimal study-time allocation strategy it implies?
> **A:** The region of proximal learning (Metcalfe, 2002) is the set of items of intermediate difficulty — items that are neither already mastered nor too difficult to learn within available time. The optimal allocation strategy directs study time preferentially to items in this region, not to the easiest items (already known) or the most difficult (insufficient return on effort). This represents a refinement of the Nelson–Narrens control channel.
> **Source:** Section IV
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #study-time-allocation #control #metcalfe

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Connection: Neural Substrates of Metacognition
> **A:** **Q:** What neural evidence supports the Nelson–Narrens claim that the meta-level is functionally distinct from the object-level?
> **A:** Lesion studies and neuroimaging research (Fleming, Lau) identify the anterior prefrontal cortex (Brodmann area 10) as selectively involved in metacognitive monitoring. Damage to this region impairs metacognitive sensitivity (the ability to distinguish correct from incorrect responses) while leaving first-order task performance intact — a dissociation that supports the functional distinctness of the meta-level.
> **Source:** Section VIII
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #neuroscience #prefrontal-cortex #fleming

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[nelson-narrens-metacognitive-model-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Original: Calibration–Regulation Coupling Thesis
> **A:** **Q:** What does the Calibration–Regulation Coupling Thesis propose about the relationship between monitoring accuracy types and control decisions?
> **A:** The thesis proposes that calibration (absolute accuracy) maps primarily onto threshold control decisions (e.g., when to stop studying an item), while resolution (relative accuracy) maps primarily onto allocation control decisions (e.g., which items to prioritise for further study). Different types of monitoring training are therefore needed for different types of control problems.
> **Source:** Section VI
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #original-synthesis #calibration #control

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 1 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What are the two main components of Zimmerman's Forethought Phase, and what sub-processes does each contain?
> **A:** Task Analysis (goal-setting and strategic planning) and Self-Motivation Beliefs (self-efficacy, outcome expectations, intrinsic interest, and goal orientation). Task Analysis addresses *how* to approach the task; Self-Motivation Beliefs determine *whether* and *with what quality of engagement*.
> **Source:** Phase II
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #srl, #forethought-phase, #zimmerman

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 2 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the key difference between a process goal and an outcome goal in SRL, and why does it matter for self-reflection?
> **A:** A process goal specifies a learning strategy or quality of engagement; an outcome goal specifies an end product or metric of completion. Process goals create evaluable criteria for self-reflection — "did I execute this strategy, and how effectively?" — whereas outcome goals yield only binary evaluation — "done / not done" — which is informationally poor for learning improvement.
> **Source:** Phase II
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #srl, #goal-setting, #process-goals, #self-reflection

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 3 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What are the two sub-processes of the Self-Judgment component of Self-Reflection, and what does each do?
> **A:** Self-evaluation (comparing performance against the standards set in Forethought — ideally against specific process goals) and causal attribution (generating an explanatory account of why the performance went as it did, ideally in terms of internal, controllable, strategy-level factors).
> **Source:** Phase III
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #srl, #self-reflection, #self-judgment

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 4 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What distinguishes an adaptive self-reaction from a defensive self-reaction in the SRL cycle?
> **A:** An adaptive reaction modifies the SRL system toward improvement: revising strategy, adjusting goal level, maintaining productive approaches. A defensive reaction protects self-esteem at the cost of regulatory information: lowering goals to guarantee success, attributing failure externally, withdrawing effort (self-handicapping). The key diagnostic: does the reaction generate actionable next steps or protect against evaluation?
> **Source:** Phase III
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #srl, #self-reaction, #adaptive-defensive

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 5 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** Why does the fluency illusion pose a specific threat to self-evaluation in the Self-Reflection Phase?
> **A:** Fluency during reading can occur through either genuine comprehension or surface pattern recognition. If comprehension monitoring is based on fluency signals, self-evaluation will reflect reading smoothness rather than actual understanding. This means self-evaluation can register "understood well" when only superficial processing occurred — generating inflated self-efficacy and no strategy revision where revision is most needed.
> **Source:** Phase V
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #fluency-illusion, #calibration, #self-reflection

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 6 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** Give an example of a maladaptive attribution for a failed self-reflection goal and its adaptive reframe using the one-sentence attribution protocol.
> **A:** Maladaptive: "I didn't generate synthesis questions because the text was too complex." Adaptive reframe: "The strategy I used was passive reading without section-pause points. It was insufficient because synthesis questions require active processing at transition points. A better approach next time would be to set a specific trigger for pausing (e.g., at every [!reflection] callout) and force question generation before continuing."
> **Source:** Phase VI-VII
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #attribution, #self-reflection, #adaptive-attribution, #protocol

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 7 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is a generative calibration test, and why is it superior to a recognition-based comprehension check?
> **A:** A generative calibration test requires the learner to produce something the text did not provide — a novel example, a mechanism reconstruction from memory, a cross-domain connection — rather than recognising what the text said. It is superior because genuine comprehension (not fluency) is required to generate novel production; a recognition check can be passed through fluency alone.
> **Source:** Phase V
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #calibration, #generative-testing, #comprehension

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 8 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three key outputs of the Self-Reflection Phase that feed into the next Forethought Phase?
> **A:** (1) Revised strategy knowledge — updated information about which strategies work, under which conditions, with which material types. (2) Calibrated self-efficacy — mastery experiences (verified by genuine performance, not fluency) or accurate gap-detection that informs efficacy calibration. (3) Revised goal architecture — adjustments to process goal ambition level based on consistent patterns of met/unmet goals.
> **Source:** Phase IV
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #srl, #feedback-loop, #self-reflection, #forethought

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 9 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between mastery grammar and performance grammar in self-reflection prompts? Give an example of each for the same evaluative moment.
> **A:** Performance grammar frames evaluation as judgment against a standard ("How well did I do?"). Mastery grammar frames evaluation as diagnosis of learning progress ("What do I understand better now than I did at the start, and where is the growing edge?"). Example: Performance — "I didn't do well enough on the synthesis goals." Mastery — "The synthesis goal revealed that I can identify connections but cannot yet reconstruct causal mechanisms from memory — that's the specific capability to develop."
> **Source:** Phase VI
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #achievement-goal-theory, #mastery-grammar, #formative-assessment

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 10 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does the Nelson-Narens monitoring-control model illuminate the relationship between the Forethought and Self-Reflection phases?
> **A:** Both phases are meta-level operations: Forethought is prospective metacognitive control (the meta level issuing goals and strategic instructions to the object level before performance) and Self-Reflection is retrospective monitoring followed by prospective control adjustment (the meta level assessing what happened at the object level and recalibrating future instructions). The phases are the two active regulatory moments of the monitoring-control cycle.
> **Source:** Phase V
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #nelson-narens, #monitoring-control, #srl

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 11 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three feedback loop failure modes identified in this report, and what diagnostic question identifies each?
> **A:** (1) Ratchet Effect — goals escalate faster than capability; diagnostic: "Do you ever genuinely celebrate that a session's process goals were fully met?" (2) Defensive Floor — goals are implicitly set low to guarantee success; diagnostic: "Do your process goals ever fail? If never, why not?" (3) Attribution Short-Circuit — accurate evaluation but uncontrollable attribution; diagnostic: "Does your attribution produce a specific actionable next step, or only a situational explanation?"
> **Source:** Phase IV
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #srl, #self-reflection, #feedback-loop, #failure-modes

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 12 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is the hierarchical goal architecture for SRL, and what is the function of each level?
> **A:** Three levels: (1) Distal Learning Goal — the broad capability being developed over months; provides directional motivation and frames the learning agenda. (2) Session Outcome Goal — what will be produced this session; defines scope and provides a milestone. (3) Proximal Process Goals — the specific strategies to be enacted during performance; these are most important for learning quality and are the primary targets of self-evaluation. The levels function together: the distal provides direction, the outcome provides scope, the process goals provide operational guidance.
> **Source:** Phase II
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #srl, #goal-architecture, #process-goals, #forethought

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 13 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What are the four self-motivation belief structures in the Forethought Phase, and what regulatory function does each serve?
> **A:** (1) Self-efficacy — beliefs about capacity to execute strategies; determines challenge level attempted, persistence, and resilience. (2) Outcome expectations — beliefs about what consequences will follow from performance; determines whether the task feels worth attempting. (3) Intrinsic interest — genuine curiosity and task value; the most durable motivational fuel, least susceptible to setback. (4) Goal orientation — the implicit purpose framework (mastery vs. performance); determines how all subsequent monitoring and evaluation data is processed.
> **Source:** Phase II
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #srl, #motivation, #forethought, #self-efficacy

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-selfreflection-pkb-report-2026-03-24]])*
> **Q:** Seed 14 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does the SRL feedback loop function as a metacognitive calibration training system over time?
> **A:** Each cycle: Forethought specifies monitoring targets (process goals), performance monitoring generates data, self-reflection evaluates monitoring signals against actual performance outcomes (the calibration check). When monitoring signals mismatch performance outcomes (felt understanding ≠ generative test performance), that discrepancy is calibration training. Over many cycles, monitoring signals become more accurate predictors of actual performance — meta-level model quality improves.
> **Source:** Phase IV-V
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #calibration, #srl, #self-regulated-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the testing effect, and why does it produce better retention than re-studying?
> **A:** The testing effect is the finding that retrieving information from long-term memory produces stronger, more durable traces than equivalent re-studying. It works because: (1) retrieval builds the retrieval pathway specifically; (2) elaborative retrieval enriches encoding; (3) failed retrieval creates error-correction opportunities; (4) it is a desirable difficulty that engages consolidation processes.
> **Source:** Section 4, [[Testing-Effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fluency illusion, and why is it a metacognitive problem?
> **A:** The fluency illusion is using processing ease (how smoothly text is read) as a cue for comprehension depth (how deeply it has been understood). It is a metacognitive monitoring error: the wrong signal (fluency) is feeding the judgment-of-learning system, producing systematic overconfidence in understanding and premature termination of study.
> **Source:** Sections 1 and 7, [[Fluency-Illusion]]
> **Difficulty:** Basic

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is a desirable difficulty, and what distinguishes it from undesirable difficulty?
> **A:** A desirable difficulty impedes performance during acquisition but produces better long-term retention and transfer by engaging constructive and reconstructive memory operations (retrieval, elaboration, discrimination). Undesirable difficulty impedes performance *without* engaging these operations — e.g., cramped fonts, poor organization, cognitive overload beyond competence zone.
> **Source:** Section 2, [[Desirable-Difficulties]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** How do spacing and retrieval practice interact, and why is the interaction important?
> **A:** Retrieval practice is substantially more powerful when combined with spacing: testing at a delay requires actual retrieval from long-term memory (implicit retrieval practice embedded in spaced study), while testing immediately draws on working memory. The combination — retrieval at optimally spaced intervals — is the theoretical foundation of spaced repetition systems.
> **Source:** Sections 4 and 5, [[Spaced-Repetition]], [[Retrieval-Practice]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the interleaving effect, and why does it feel less productive than blocked study?
> **A:** The interleaving effect is that mixing problem types or topics within a study session produces better long-term retention and transfer than studying each type exhaustively before moving to the next. It feels less productive because it prevents the shallow repetition fluency that blocked study generates — requiring re-encoding from scratch on each encounter, which is the source of both the felt difficulty and the learning benefit.
> **Source:** Section 5, [[Interleaving-Effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** According to Self-Determination Theory, what are the three basic psychological needs and their relevance to academic reading engagement?
> **A:** Autonomy (experiencing reading as self-chosen, identity-aligned), Competence (experiencing genuine mastery growth through challenge calibrated to current ability), and Relatedness (experiencing connection to intellectual community and purpose). All three must be satisfied for reading engagement to be sustainable and autonomously motivated rather than compliance-driven and fragile.
> **Source:** Section 8, [[Basic-Psychological-Needs]], [[Self-Determination-Theory]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the generation effect, and how does it differ from retrieval practice?
> **A:** The generation effect is the finding that self-generated information is encoded more durably than received information. Unlike retrieval practice (which retrieves already-encoded information), generation occurs *during initial encoding* — before durable storage. Both engage active construction, but at different points in the learning cycle: generation at encoding, retrieval practice during maintenance.
> **Source:** Section 3, [[Generation-Effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the self-explanation effect, and what is its primary mechanism?
> **A:** Students who generate more self-explanations during problem-solving and text study (articulating why claims are true, how processes work) show substantially greater learning gains than those who explain less. The primary mechanism is gap-detection: self-explanation forces the learner to confront logical gaps in their understanding that fluent reading conceals, triggering targeted repair of exactly the incomplete aspects of their mental model.
> **Source:** Section 6, [[Self-Explanation-Effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three phases of Zimmerman's cyclical self-regulated learning model, and what is their relevance to strategic reading?
> **A:** Forethought (goal-setting, prior knowledge activation, strategy planning before reading), Performance (active strategy deployment with comprehension monitoring during reading), and Self-Reflection (honest post-reading evaluation of what was learned and strategy effectiveness). Most natural readers skip forethought and self-reflection entirely, which eliminates the cyclical improvement mechanism that makes SRL more effective over time.
> **Source:** Section 10, [[Cyclical-Model-of-Self-Regulated-Learning]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** In the Nelson-Narens model of metacognition, what are monitoring and control, and how do they interact?
> **A:** Monitoring: information flowing from the object (cognitive) level to the meta level, including judgments of learning, feelings of knowing, and comprehension signals. Control: commands flowing from the meta level to the object level, adjusting strategies, allocating study time, deciding to review. Effective self-regulation requires both: monitoring that generates accurate signals, and control operations that respond to those signals adaptively.
> **Source:** Section 7, [[Nelson-Narens-Model]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced



















































































































## Key Figures & Intellectual Lineage

> [!person] **Barry Zimmerman (1942–2017)** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]])*
> **Core Contribution:** Developer of the Cyclical Model of Self-Regulated Learning, extending Bandura's social cognitive framework into the domain of learning and academic performance. His empirical program demonstrated that self-regulatory processes were teachable, measurable, and predictive of academic achievement.
> **Role in This Analysis:** Primary architect — the Forethought and Self-Reflection phases analyzed throughout this report are Zimmerman's constructs.
> **Relationship to Other Figures:** Extended Bandura's social cognitive theory; influenced by Vygotsky's zone of proximal development; his framework has been compared and integrated with Pintrich's four-column SRL matrix.
> **Key Works:** *Self-Regulated Learning and Academic Achievement* (2001); "A Social Cognitive View of Self-Regulated Academic Learning" (1989).

> [!person] **Albert Bandura (1925–2021)** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]])*
> **Core Contribution:** Developer of Social Cognitive Theory and the self-efficacy construct. His four sources of self-efficacy (mastery experiences, vicarious experience, social persuasion, physiological states) ground the motivational beliefs subsystem of Zimmerman's Forethought Phase.
> **Role in This Analysis:** Primary theorist for the self-efficacy and motivational architecture of the Forethought Phase (Part II, VIII).
> **Key Works:** *Social Foundations of Thought and Action* (1986); *Self-Efficacy: The Exercise of Control* (1997).

> [!person] **Bernard Weiner (1935–present)** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]])*
> **Core Contribution:** Developer of Attribution Theory in educational contexts, providing the dimensional analysis (locus, stability, controllability) that is the foundation of the Self-Reflection Phase's attribution analysis component.
> **Role in This Analysis:** Primary theorist for the causal attribution component of Self-Judgment in Part IV.
> **Key Works:** *Human Motivation: Metaphors, Theories, and Research* (1992); "An Attributional Theory of Motivation and Emotion" (1985).

> [!person] **Carol Dweck (1946–present)** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]])*
> **Core Contribution:** Developer of the framework distinguishing entity theories (intelligence is fixed) from incremental theories (intelligence is malleable), and the mastery/performance goal orientation distinction.
> **Role in This Analysis:** Grounds the motivational language design in Part VIII and the attribution analysis in Part IV — the shift from stable-internal to unstable-controllable attributions is the practical implementation of incremental theory.
> **Key Works:** *Mindset: The New Psychology of Success* (2006); *Self-Theories: Their Role in Motivation, Personality, and Development* (1999).

> [!person] **Paul Black & Dylan Wiliam** (active 1990s–present)** *(from [[formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]])*
> **Core Contribution:** The 1998 synthesis "Inside the Black Box" established the empirical case for formative assessment's large effect sizes and identified the classroom practices most strongly associated with gains. Their formulation — that assessment for learning fundamentally differs from assessment of learning in its feedback architecture — is the foundational distinction in the field.
> 
> **Relationship to Other Figures:** Built on Sadler's (1989) analysis of feedback and the gap between desired and actual performance. Their work influenced Wiliam's later empirical program on implementation and Hattie's visible learning meta-analyses.
> 
> **Key Works:** *Inside the Black Box* (1998); *Assessment for Learning* (Wiliam, 2011)
> 
> **Relevance to This Analysis:** Their finding that effect sizes vary substantially across studies, and that practices requiring active student engagement with their own knowledge gaps outperform passive information delivery, is the primary empirical motivation for the MCM.

> [!person] **John Hattie** (active 1980s–present)** *(from [[formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]])*
> **Core Contribution:** The Visible Learning program — synthesizing over 800 meta-analyses of educational interventions — provides the largest systematic evidence base on what works in education. Feedback ranks among the top predictors of achievement.
> 
> **Relationship to Other Figures:** The [[Hattie-&-Timperley-Feedback-Model]] (2007) is the most theoretically developed account of why different types of feedback produce different outcomes, directly relevant to the MCM's argument that self-regulation-level feedback outperforms task-level feedback.
> 
> **Key Works:** *Visible Learning* (2009); "The Power of Feedback" with Timperley (2007)
> 
> **Relevance to This Analysis:** The four-level feedback taxonomy provides empirical support for the MCM's prediction that practices that build monitoring infrastructure (self-regulation level) produce larger gains than practices that merely deliver information (task level).

> [!person] **Thomas Nelson & Louis Narens** (active 1980s–1990s)** *(from [[formative-assessment-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]])*
> **Core Contribution:** The monitoring-control framework (1990) provided the foundational architecture for understanding metacognition as a two-level system with specific structural properties. Monitoring generates meta-level representations; control regulates object-level processing based on those representations.
> 
> **Relationship to Other Figures:** Built on Flavell's metacognition work; influenced Dunlosky, Metcalfe, and the entire metacognitive calibration research program.
> 
> **Key Works:** "Metamemory: A Theoretical Framework and New Findings" (1990, Psychological Review)
> 
> **Relevance to This Analysis:** The Nelson-Narens architecture is the explanatory foundation of the MCM. The monitoring-control distinction is what allows the MCM to specify *where* formative assessment does its work.

> [!person] **Edwin Locke (b. 1938) & Gary Latham (b. 1945)** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Founders of [[Goal-Setting-Theory|goal-setting theory]]. Their 35-year programmatic research collaboration, spanning over 1,000 studies, established that specific, difficult goals enhance performance through direction, effort, persistence, and strategy-development mechanisms. Key works: *A Theory of Goal Setting and Task Performance* (1990), "Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting and Task Motivation" (2002). Relationship to other figures: their work provides the specificity-and-difficulty mechanism that SDT qualifies with motivational quality.

> [!person] **Edward Deci (1942–2022) & Richard Ryan (b. 1953)** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Co-founders of [[Self-Determination-Theory]]. Developed the taxonomy of motivational regulation (intrinsic, identified, introjected, external), the basic psychological needs framework ([[Autonomy-Need|autonomy]], [[Competence-Need|competence]], [[Relatedness-Need|relatedness]]), and six mini-theories including cognitive evaluation theory, organismic integration theory, and goal contents theory. Key works: *Intrinsic Motivation and Self-Determination in Human Behavior* (1985), *Self-Determination and Intrinsic Motivation in Human Behavior* (2000). Relationship: their autonomy framework provides the central qualifying condition for Locke and Latham's specificity mechanism.

> [!person] **Carol Dweck (b. 1946)** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Developer of implicit theories of intelligence and the [[Growth-Mindset|growth/fixed mindset]] framework. Her research demonstrated that children's *theories* of intelligence — incremental vs. entity — determine their goal orientations, persistence patterns, and responses to failure. Key works: *Self-Theories* (1999), *Mindset* (2006). Relationship: her work on learning goals vs. performance goals was seminal for [[Achievement-Goal-Theory]] and was extended by Elliot's 2×2 framework.

> [!person] **Andrew Elliot (b. 1962)** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Architect of the 2×2 achievement goal framework crossing mastery/performance with approach/avoidance. This taxonomic refinement resolved inconsistencies in earlier achievement goal research and revealed that performance-approach goals can sometimes enhance achievement (though not deep learning). Key work: "A 2 × 2 Achievement Goal Framework" (2001, with McGregor). Relationship: extended Dweck and Ames's binary into a four-cell model with greater predictive precision.

> [!person] **Barry Zimmerman (1942–2020)** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Developer of the tripartite [[Zimmerman-SRL-Model|SRL model]] (forethought → performance → self-reflection) and leading theorist of self-regulation as a learnable skill. His work demonstrated that expert self-regulators differ from novices not in cognitive ability but in the quality of their regulatory processes. Key works: "Self-Regulated Learning and Academic Achievement" (1990, 2000). Relationship: his SRL cycle provides the process architecture within which Locke-Latham's goal mechanisms and Deci-Ryan's motivational dynamics operate.

> [!person] **Paul Pintrich (1953–2003)** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Developer of the 4×4 SRL framework crossing phases (forethought, monitoring, control, reflection) with areas (cognition, motivation, behaviour, context). His insight that self-regulation encompasses motivational and contextual regulation — not just cognitive and behavioural — expanded the design space for learning-support systems. Key works: "The Role of Goal Orientation in Self-Regulated Learning" (2000). Relationship: extended Zimmerman's model with the motivational regulation dimension critical for PKM.

> [!person] **John Flavell (b. 1928)** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Pioneer of [[Metacognition|metacognition]], coining the term and proposing the tripartite model of metacognitive knowledge (person, task, strategy). His framework provides the vocabulary for understanding what learners know about their own cognitive processes and how that knowledge guides self-regulation. Key work: "Metacognition and Cognitive Monitoring" (1979). Relationship: his taxonomy of metacognitive knowledge complements Nelson & Narens's process model of monitoring and control.

> [!person] **Thomas Nelson (1943–2005) & Louis Narens** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Developers of the [[Nelson-Narens-Model|monitoring-control framework]] of metacognition, distinguishing meta-level from object-level with information flowing in two directions: monitoring (object → meta) and control (meta → object). This model provides the computational architecture for understanding how learners generate and use self-assessment data. Key work: "Metamemory: A Theoretical Framework and New Findings" (1990). Relationship: their framework is the mechanistic complement to Flavell's taxonomic approach.

> [!person] **Paul Black & Dylan Wiliam** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Architects of the [[Formative-Assessment|formative assessment]] research programme demonstrating that assessment-for-learning — assessment designed to inform ongoing instruction and learning rather than certify attainment — produces substantial learning gains. Key work: "Inside the Black Box" (1998), "Developing the Theory of Formative Assessment" (2009). Relationship: their formative assessment principles translate directly into the monitoring and reflective-assessment layers of PKM design.

> [!person] **Edwin Locke (b. 1938) & Gary Latham (b. 1944)** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Primary Contribution**: Developed and validated [[Goal-Setting-Theory]] across 400+ studies from the late 1960s to present. Established that specific, challenging goals consistently produce higher performance than vague or easy goals, mediated by direction, effort, persistence, and strategy activation.
> **Key Works**: *A Theory of Goal Setting and Task Performance* (1990); "Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting and Task Motivation" (2002, *American Psychologist*)
> **Intellectual Legacy**: Framework is the most replicated finding in industrial-organizational psychology; its integration with SRL research by Zimmerman, Pintrich, and Schunk produced the educational applications central to this report.

> [!person] **Barry Zimmerman (b. 1942)** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Primary Contribution**: Developed the [[Zimmerman's-Cyclical-Model-of-Self-Regulated-Learning|Cyclical SRL Model]] and its three-phase architecture; conducted foundational research on the development of SRL in educational contexts; extended Bandura's social-cognitive framework into a complete regulatory model.
> **Key Works**: "Self-Regulated Learning and Academic Achievement" (1990); "A Social Cognitive View of Self-Regulated Academic Learning" (1989); *Becoming a Self-Regulated Learner* (2002)
> **Relationship to this report**: The structural backbone of Sections 3, 7, and 8.

> [!person] **Paul Pintrich (1953–2003)** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Primary Contribution**: Developed [[Pintrich's-Integrative-SRL-Framework|Pintrich's integrated SRL framework]], extending Zimmerman's model to four regulatory areas (cognition, motivation/affect, behavior, context); conducted influential research on achievement goals and SRL in higher education.
> **Key Works**: "A Conceptual Framework for Assessing Motivation and Self-Regulated Learning in College Students" (2004); *Motivation in Education* (with Schunk); contributed the Motivated Strategies for Learning Questionnaire (MSLQ).
> **Legacy**: Most widely used SRL research measurement instrument (MSLQ); theoretical framework integrating motivation and metacognition.

> [!person] **Peter Gollwitzer (b. 1950)** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Primary Contribution**: Developed the Rubicon Model of action phases and the [[Implementation-Intention]] construct; provided the empirical foundation for volitional control in the intention-action gap literature.
> **Key Works**: "Implementation Intentions" (1999, *American Psychologist*); *Volition and Personality* (with Heckhausen, 1991).
> **Intellectual lineage**: Built on Heckhausen's action control research; Gollwitzer's implementation intention framework has generated 200+ empirical studies across health, education, and performance domains.

> [!person] **Albert Bandura (b. 1925)** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Primary Contribution**: Developed [[Self-Efficacy-Theory]] and social-cognitive learning theory; provided the motivational foundation that Zimmerman applied to the SRL framework.
> **Key Works**: *Social Foundations of Thought and Action* (1986); *Self-Efficacy: The Exercise of Control* (1997).

> [!person] **Carol Dweck (b. 1946)** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Primary Contribution**: Developed the [[Growth-Mindset|incremental/entity theory of intelligence]] framework; linked implicit theories of intelligence to achievement goal orientations and their regulatory consequences.
> **Key Works**: *Mindset* (2006); "Motivational Processes Affecting Learning" (1986, *American Psychologist*).

> [!person] **John Flavell (1928–)** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Stanford University. Developmental psychologist who coined the term "metacognition" in 1976 and developed the foundational framework distinguishing metacognitive knowledge (person, task, strategy variables) from metacognitive experiences. All subsequent metacognition research builds on Flavell's conceptual foundation.
> 
> **Relationship to other figures:** Provided the conceptual vocabulary that Brown, Nelson, and Schraw elaborated into more precise models. Flavell's work was primarily taxonomic; others developed process models.
> 
> **Key work:** Flavell, J. H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring. *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906-911.
> 
> **See also:** [[John-Flavell]], [[Flavell]], [[Flavell-Metacognition-Framework]], [[Flavell's-Metacognitive-Framework]]

> [!person] **Ann Brown (1943–1999)** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> University of California, Berkeley. Distinguished between knowledge of cognition and regulation of cognition — a distinction that operationalised metacognition for educational research and became the standard framework for intervention design. Pioneered reciprocal teaching as a metacognitive instructional strategy.
> 
> **Relationship to other figures:** Complemented Flavell's taxonomy with an emphasis on executive regulation. Her work directly influenced Schraw's tripartite model and Zimmerman's SRL framework.
> 
> **Key works:** Brown, A. L. (1987). Metacognition, executive control, self-regulation, and other more mysterious mechanisms. In F. E. Weinert & R. H. Kluwe (Eds.), *Metacognition, Motivation, and Understanding*.
> 
> **See also:** [[Ann-Brown]], [[Knowledge-of-Cognition]], [[Regulation-of-Cognition]]

> [!person] **Thomas O. Nelson (1941–2005) & Louis Narens** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> University of Maryland. Developed the meta-level/object-level model of metacognitive monitoring and control — the most influential process model in metacognition research. Identified the bidirectional information flow (monitoring upward, control downward) that structures all subsequent analyses of metacognitive dynamics.
> 
> **Relationship to other figures:** Provided the computational/architectural framework that operationalised Brown's distinction between monitoring and regulation. Direct influence on calibration research.
> 
> **Key work:** Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. *Psychology of Learning and Motivation*, 26, 125-173.
> 
> **See also:** [[Nelson-Narens-Model]], [[The-Nelson-Narens-Monitoring-Control-Model]], [[Meta-Level-Object-Level-Model]]

> [!person] **Gregory Schraw (1957–2016)** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> University of Nevada, Las Vegas. Synthesised prior work into a tripartite model of metacognitive knowledge (declarative, procedural, conditional) that is widely used in educational research and intervention design. Also developed the Metacognitive Awareness Inventory (MAI), one of the most widely used self-report measures of metacognition.
> 
> **Relationship to other figures:** Built on Flavell's taxonomy and Brown's operational distinction, adding the critical category of conditional knowledge — knowing *when and why* to use particular strategies.
> 
> **Key work:** Schraw, G. (1998). Promoting general metacognitive awareness. *Instructional Science*, 26, 113-125.
> 
> **See also:** [[Gregory-Schraw]], [[Metacognitive-Knowledge]], [[Metacognitive-Awareness-Inventory]]

> [!person] **Barry Zimmerman (1942–2014)** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> City University of New York. Developed the cyclical model of self-regulated learning (forethought → performance → self-reflection) that became the most widely applied SRL framework. Integrated metacognitive, motivational, and behavioural self-regulation within a unified developmental model.
> 
> **Relationship to other figures:** Extended Brown's focus on regulation into a full process model; complementary to Pintrich's four-column framework. Together, Zimmerman and Pintrich defined the field of SRL research.
> 
> **Key work:** Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation*.
> 
> **See also:** [[Barry-Zimmerman]], [[Zimmerman-SRL-Model]], [[Self-Regulated-Learning]]

> [!person] **Paul Pintrich (1953–2003)** *(from [[metacognition-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> University of Michigan. Developed the integrative SRL framework crossing four phases (forethought, monitoring, control, reflection) with four areas (cognition, motivation/affect, behaviour, context). Uniquely emphasised the motivational and contextual dimensions of self-regulation that purely metacognitive models neglect.
> 
> **Relationship to other figures:** Complemented Zimmerman's cyclical model by adding explicit motivational and contextual dimensions. Extended Brown's regulation concept beyond purely cognitive control.
> 
> **Key work:** Pintrich, P. R. (2000). The role of goal orientation in self-regulated learning. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation*.
> 
> **See also:** [[Pintrich]], [[Paul-Pintrich]], [[Self-Regulated-Learning]]

> [!person] **John H. Flavell (1928–)** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> John Flavell is the developmental psychologist whose 1979 and 1981 papers established the theoretical foundation for the systematic study of metacognitive knowledge. Trained in Piagetian developmental psychology, Flavell applied constructivist developmental methods to the question of how children understand and monitor their own cognitive processes, producing a taxonomy (person-task-strategy knowledge) that structured subsequent research for decades. His influence extends to the theory of mind literature (his work on perspective-taking and children's understanding of mental states) and to reading comprehension research (his studies of comprehension monitoring failure in young readers).
> 
> **Lineage:** Influenced by [[Jean-Piaget]]'s constructivist developmental framework and [[Ann-L.-Brown|Ann Brown]]'s parallel work on reading comprehension.
> 
> **Relationship to Report:** Provides the foundational taxonomy (Phase II.1) and the theoretical frame within which subsequent elaborations are situated.

> [!person] **Ann L. Brown (1943–1999)** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> A developmental and educational psychologist at the University of California, Berkeley, Brown made foundational contributions to the metacognitive knowledge literature through her studies of reading comprehension, memory development, and cognitive strategy instruction. Her distinction between knowledge *of* cognition and regulation *of* cognition has been as influential as Flavell's taxonomy, providing the conceptual framework that most educational interventions have used. Her work on reciprocal teaching — a pedagogical approach for scaffolded metacognitive strategy instruction — is among the most replicated educational interventions in the literature.
> 
> **Lineage:** A contemporary and occasional collaborator of [[John-H.-Flavell|Flavell]]; influenced by Soviet activity theory and Vygotskian accounts of mediated learning.
> 
> **Relationship to Report:** Provides the knowledge-regulation distinction (Phase III.2) that is central to the report's argument about the Knowledge-Activation Gap.

> [!person] **Gregory Schraw (1960s–)** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> Schraw's contributions to metacognitive knowledge research have been primarily synthesizing and operationalizing. His 1994 Metacognitive Awareness Inventory (with Dennison) is the most widely used self-report measure of metacognitive knowledge in educational research. His 1995 theoretical synthesis with Moshman organized the field's conceptual frameworks, and his empirical work demonstrated that metacognitive knowledge predicts academic outcomes across domains after controlling for prior achievement.
> 
> **Lineage:** Builds on [[John-H.-Flavell|Flavell]] and [[Ann-L.-Brown|Brown]]; influenced by Paris, Lipson, and Wixson's declarative-procedural-conditional framework.
> 
> **Relationship to Report:** Provides the declarative-procedural-conditional taxonomy (Phase II.2) and the primary empirical evidence base (Phase III.5) for the educational significance of metacognitive knowledge.

> [!person] **Thomas O. Nelson & Louis Narens (1940s–/1940s–)** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> Nelson and Narens's monitoring-control model (1990, 1994) constitutes the most precise computational-level account of how metacognitive processes interact with object-level cognition. Nelson's experimental program — developing the methodology for studying judgments of learning, feeling of knowing, and metacognitive control — provided the empirical foundation for the theoretical architecture that he and Narens formalized together.
> 
> **Lineage:** Influenced by Herbert Simon's information-processing framework and Jacoby's experiments on memory confidence.
> 
> **Relationship to Report:** Provides the monitoring-control hierarchy (Phase III.3) that specifies the functional role of metacognitive knowledge in the regulatory architecture.

> [!person] **Anastasia Efklides (1948–)** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> Greek cognitive-educational psychologist whose Metacognitive and Affective Model of Self-Regulated Learning (MASRL) and systematic analysis of metacognitive feelings constitute the primary theoretical account of the phenomenological dimension of metacognitive processing. Efklides's contribution is the specification of metacognitive feelings as a distinct construct — online, affective, situationally generated — that bridges stored metacognitive knowledge and regulatory action.
> 
> **Lineage:** Builds on [[John-H.-Flavell|Flavell]]'s original inclusion of metacognitive experiences as a fourth component of the metacognitive system; influenced by Norbert Schwarz's feelings-as-information framework.
> 
> **Relationship to Report:** Provides the metacognitive feelings construct (Phase III.4) and the insight that monitoring calibration requires not only accurate stored knowledge but accurately calibrated online feeling states.

> [!person] **Scott Paris, Marjorie Lipson & Karen Wixson (1980s)** *(from [[metacognitive-knowledge-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> The Paris, Lipson, and Wixson (1983) paper is the primary source of the declarative-procedural-conditional knowledge taxonomy as applied to metacognitive knowledge and reading strategy instruction. Their work, grounded in the reading comprehension literature, extended the philosophical knowing-that/knowing-how distinction into the domain of metacognitive knowledge and introduced the conditional dimension as a theoretically and practically distinct type.
> 
> **Relationship to Report:** Foundational for the declarative-procedural-conditional framework developed in Phase II.2.

> [!person] **John Dewey (1859–1952) — The Architectural Ancestor** *(from [[metacognitive-reflection-foundational-report-2026-04-09]])*
> Dewey's *How We Think* (1910, revised 1933) provides the first systematic psychological account of what he called "reflective thinking" — the active, persistent, and careful consideration of any belief or supposed form of knowledge in the light of the grounds that support it and the further conclusions to which it tends. Dewey distinguished reflective from impulsive thinking, not by content but by structure: reflective thinking passes through stages of perplexity, active examination, and hypothetical reasoning toward resolution.
